## 2026-11-05 — #23 *Psiloscops flammeolus* v5 — one-week known-age feather boundary

### FLOW-RICHMOND-DEWEESE-PILLMORE-1980-P35-46
- **Title / authors / year:** *Brief Observations on the Breeding Biology of the Flammulated Owl in Colorado* — Merle L. Richmond, Lawrence R. DeWeese & Richard E. Pillmore, 1980.
- **URL / type / pages / accessed:** https://journal.westernfieldornithologists.org/index.php/westernbirds/article/download/305/297/582 ; primary field and captive-development paper, *Western Birds* 11:35–46; printed pp.35–46; accessed 2026-11-05.
- **Species / stages / facts:** historical name *Otus flammeolus*, current *Psiloscops flammeolus*. Printed p.37 reports reused cavities without obvious nest material except wood bits. Printed pp.40 and 44 describe/photograph young at about one and three weeks: at about one week dense down remains, white except for a few darkening primary feathers emerging as blood quills, with no evident tail; by three weeks the bird is generally gray, body juvenal-feathered, barred below, with developed wing/tail feathers but still-short tail. Printed p.44 also bounds behavior: around 25 d captive young could fly 0.5 m downward but not upward; directed flight was much later.
- **Limits / confidence:** direct species-specific evidence but sample was small, two photographed captive nestlings had uncertain exact ages and may have been developmentally retarded after starvation; do not turn “about one week” into an exact day or universal sequence. High confidence for the visible one-week boundary, moderate for timing generalization.
- **Design decision:** v5 selects the approximately one-week photographed stage: dense white down, only a few short dark primary blood quills, no tail, low fully supported in sparse cavity wood bits. No flight or standing inference.
- **Links:** `research/species/23-psiloscops-flammeolus-v5.md`; `production/runs/20261105T013100+0800-cycle-1.md`; planned `design/concepts/23-psiloscops-flammeolus-v5.png`.

### FLOW-BC-RISC-HAUSLEITNER-2006-P36
- **Title / author / year:** *Inventory Methods for Owl Surveys*, Standards for Components of British Columbia's Biodiversity No. 42 — Doris Hausleitner / B.C. Ministry of Environment, revised draft 2006.
- **URL / type / page / accessed:** https://sc.arcabc.ca/_flysystem/repo-bin/2019-02/sc_3947.pdf ; government survey standard / synthesis; printed p.36; accessed 2026-11-05.
- **Fact / use:** adult 15–17 cm and dark-eyed; it states natal down is replaced by juvenile plumage in the first 10 days, citing McCallum 1994. This conflicts with the former v4 design phrase “11-day mostly white natal down” and triggers correction toward a directly photographed one-week stage.
- **Limits / confidence:** secondary synthesis, not a daily photographic growth series; the exact “first 10 days” wording should not override the primary paper's sample limitations or be treated as universal to every individual/population.

### Reuse, conflict correction and unknowns
- Reused: `FLOW-POWERS-1996-JRR` for cavity dimensions/phenology (printed pp.15–20; no day-specific plumage), plus v4 institutional adult sources `FLOW-CORNELL-ID-2026`, `FLOW-CORNELL-LIFE-2026`, `FLOW-ORI-2026`.
- Corrected boundary: v4's 11-day “mostly white natal down” claim depended on a non-peer-reviewed account and is not retained. V5 uses the original 1980 species-specific photographed approximately one-week stage.
- Unknown: exact day within “about one week,” individual/population variance, eye-opening timing, blood-quill count/length, quantified sex-size ratio, universal cavity geometry and continuous daily feather chronology.
- Source accounting: new verified 2; reused 4; corrected 1 major age/plumage boundary.


## 2026-11-05 — #24 *Glaucidium californicum* v4 — recently fledged dependency boundary

### NPO-DESHLER-MURPHY-2012-CONDOR-P314-322
- **Title / authors / year:** *The Breeding Biology of the Northern Pygmy Owl: Do the Smallest of the Small Have an Advantage?* — John F. Deshler & Michael T. Murphy, 2012.
- **DOI / URL / type / pages / accessed:** https://doi.org/10.1525/cond.2012.100140 ; https://academic.oup.com/condor/article/114/2/314/5152768 ; primary breeding study, *The Condor* 114(2):314–322; accessed 2026-11-05.
- **Scope / facts:** 24 initial nests in northwestern Oregon, 2007–2009. Incubation about 30 d; nestling period averaged 27 d; broods left over 2.3±0.2 d (range 1.5–5). Small females sometimes bred earlier and fledged more young; the paper does not justify converting body-size patterns into a fixed standing-height ratio.
- **Limits / use:** paper uses broad *Glaucidium gnoma* and does not publish a daily plumage series. It supports a recently fledged, still-dependent stage and cautions against simplistic “larger female” display claims, not exact juvenile feather geometry.

### NPO-MACAULAY-588486171-2023
- **Institution / record / date:** Cornell Lab Macaulay Library ML588486171, John Patterson field video, 23 June 2023.
- **URL / type / accessed:** https://macaulaylibrary.org/asset/588486171 ; institutional field media with observer notes; accessed 2026-11-05.
- **Facts / use:** two fledglings outside a known cavity; one was a less able flyer, perched in spruce, moved outward on the branch, received food from adults and then flew. Supports branch grip, parental dependence and limited but real post-fledging movement.
- **Limits:** hatch date, exact age, sex and plumage measurements are not supplied; do not label an exact day or depict independent hunting.

### Reuse and correction
- Reused `NPO-FRYE-JAGEMAN-2012-WJO` (16 nesting attempts; adults attended broods for weeks; earliest observed hunting 9 d postfledging), `NPO-CORNELL-ID-2026`, and `NPO-CORNELL-OVERVIEW-2026`.
- V4 drops v3's over-precise visual implication that a nine-day post-fledging plumage is directly described; it keeps only the evidenced dependency/action boundary and labels the visual as a conservative recently fledged inference.
- Unknown: exact individual age/post-fledging day, regional juvenile spotting/tail-growth curve, iris transition, flight performance, modern species-limit mapping of broad *G. gnoma* studies and quantified sex-specific linear dimensions.
- Source accounting: new verified 2; reused 3; corrected 1 evidence/visual boundary.


## 2026-11-05 — #41 *Glaucidium cobanense* v5 — documented pair morph and age-unknown juvenile boundary

### GUPO-HOWELL-EISERMANN-2011-P74-76 — re-read and tightened
- **Title / authors / year:** *Guatemalan Pygmy Owl Glaucidium cobanense is a good species* — Steve N. G. Howell & Knut Eisermann, 2011.
- **URL / type / pages / accessed:** https://neotropicalbirdingandconservation.org/wp-content/uploads/2025/07/09-neotropical-birding-10-photospot-guatemalan-pygmy-owl-glaucidium-cobanense-is-a-good-species.pdf ; primary field/taxonomic article, *Neotropical Birding* 9:74–76; re-read 2026-11-05.
- **Facts:** printed p.75 Figure 2 documents a rufous morph at Chelemhá; Figure 3 documents a brown-morph male at a nest entrance there. Printed p.76 reports no appreciable size difference from *G. gnoma*, not a sex-size direction.
- **Limits / correction:** the figures do not prove that rufous is female or that morph is sex-linked. V5 retains the separately documented Chelemhá mixed-morph pair only as an individual-pair trace, keeps adults near equal, and removes any universal sex/morph implication.

### GUPO-BSG-NATURAL-HISTORY-2026 — re-read and tightened
- **Title / authors / accessed:** *Natural history of Guatemalan Pygmy-Owl (Glaucidium cobanense)* — Knut Eisermann & Claudia Avendaño / Bird Studies Guatemala; accessed 2026-11-05.
- **URL / type / sections:** https://www.bird-studies-guatemala.info/Guatemalan-Pygmy-Owl.htm ; researcher-maintained natural-history page, lines 30–73.
- **Facts:** the authors state that they documented Guatemala's first nesting records; line 44 identifies juveniles at a nest entrance; lines 67–73 identify a brown-morph juvenile and a female with juvenile. Lines 40 and 51 identify a brown-morph male; lines 58–65 show both rufous and brown adult examples.
- **Limits / correction:** captions do not give hatch date, feather chronology, iris timing, flight threshold or sex-specific linear measurements. The embedded juvenile pixels were not independently extracted this run. V5 therefore uses an age-unknown, supported brown juvenile at the cavity lip, not a day-labeled fledgling or miniature adult.

### GUPO-AOS-NACC-2013A — reused taxonomy and habitat
- **Institution / pages:** AOU/NACC proposal 2013-A-2, pp.259–263 of the proposal packet (PDF section lines 144–255), re-read 2026-11-05.
- **URL:** https://americanornithology.org/wp-content/uploads/2020/02/2013-A.pdf
- **Facts / use:** current species *G. cobanense*; historical broad treatment *G. gnoma cobanense*; humid broadleaf, pine-oak and coniferous forest/edge and shaded plantations at 1,500–3,500 m; rufous and brown morphs.
- **Limits:** no juvenile development series or quantified sex-size direction.

### V5 evidence accounting
- New stable sources: 0; re-read/reused verified sources: 3; corrected evidence boundaries: 2.
- The user-uploaded Mikkola guide was unavailable in this execution; repository-indexed page evidence was not represented as a fresh read.
- Unknown: exact juvenile age, continuous wing/tail and iris chronology, independent flight, quantified sex dimorphism, and universal nest geometry.
- Links: `research/species/41-glaucidium-cobanense-v5.md`; `production/runs/20261105T015400+0800-cycle-3.md`; planned concept v5.


## 2026-11-05 — #42 *Athene brama* v5 — 21–25-day plumage versus flight boundary

### SPOT-ALI-2015 — re-read primary developmental series
- **Title / authors / year:** *Nest trees, habitat and breeding biology of the spotted owlet Athene brama brama (Temminck, 1821) in human habitation and agricultural landscape of India* — Abdul Hameed Mohamed Samsoor Ali & Ramachandran Santhanakrishnan, 2015.
- **DOI / URL / type / pages / accessed:** https://doi.org/10.1080/21658005.2015.1061248 ; https://www.researchgate.net/publication/280385349_Nest_trees_habitat_and_breeding_biology_of_the_spotted_owletAthene_brama_bramaTemminck_1821_in_human_habitation_and_agricultural_landscape_of_India ; primary two-habitat field study, *Zoology and Ecology* 25(3):211–219, printed pp.214–216, re-read 2026-11-05.
- **Scope / facts:** 27 nests in Madurai district. Printed pp.215–216 and Figure 6 document 21–25 d young with white neck and eyebrow plumage, typical gray feathers appearing adult-like, and well-developed remiges/rectrices with white wing markings. Young looked fully adult-like at 26–30 d; fledging averaged 31.4 ± 2.67 d, range 28–33 d. Cavities were 3–15 m high; large banyan trees were commonly used.
- **Limits / correction:** adult-like feathers at 21–25 d do not establish flight or branch-leaving. V5 may show formed folded wings and early white markings, but keeps the bird cavity-supported and dependent.
- **Design use:** 21–25 d cavity-lip juvenile with shorter tail, soft rounded body and folded formed wing; no open-wing threat or flight.

### SPOT-PANDE-2011 — re-read growth and sex boundary
- **Title / authors / year:** *Biometry based ageing of nestling Indian Spotted Owlets (Athene brama brama)* — Pande et al., 2011.
- **DOI / URL / type / pages / accessed:** https://doi.org/10.3897/zookeys.132.1346 ; https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3208435/ ; primary known-age growth study, *ZooKeys* 132:75–88, methods and results, re-read 2026-11-05.
- **Scope / facts:** 53 active nests; eight known-age nestlings measured from hatching through fledging, 0–32 d, with 136 measurements. Sexes were externally alike and nestlings could not be separated by sex. The paper estimates acquisition of flight from biometry rather than external resemblance.
- **Limits / correction:** small intensive longitudinal sample and southern Indian subspecies. It supports equal external sex treatment and separating plumage maturity from locomotor ability, not a universal standing-height ratio.
- **Design use:** adults near equal; juvenile locomotion remains conservatively bounded.

### Reuse, taxonomy and unresolved items
- Reused `SPOT-NERLEKAR-ETHOGRAM-2014`, `SPOT-MIKKOLA-2013` and `SPOT-AVIBASE-2026`; the uploaded Mikkola guide was unavailable and was not represented as freshly read.
- Current name: *Athene brama*; historical names retained in repository mapping include *Carine brama* and *Noctua indica*.
- Correction: v4 selected 16–20 d to avoid mature wing pattern. V5 uses the next directly photographed 21–25 d stage but explicitly decouples adult-like plumage from flight, because fledging was later (28–33 d).
- Unknown: exact individual feather map, iris chronology, sex-specific growth, universal cavity material, and precise first-flight ability for the pictured individual.
- Source accounting: new stable sources 0; re-read/reused verified sources 5; corrected design boundaries 2.
- Links: `research/species/42-athene-brama-v5.md`; `production/runs/20261105T020900+0800-cycle-4.md`; planned concept v5.


## 2026-11-05 — #43 *Athene blewitti* v5 — 28–30-day weak-flight and iris boundary

### FOW-WRCS-EAST-KALIBHIT-2017-P26-28-45-46
- **Title / authors / year:** *A Study on the Population, Demography and Ecology of the Forest Owlet Athene (Heteroglaux) blewitti in East Kalibhit Forests, Khandwa District, Madhya Pradesh* — Prachi Mehta, Akshay V. Anand & Jayant Kulkarni, 2017.
- **URL / type / pages / accessed:** https://www.raptors.org.in/assets/pdf/projects/Ecology-in-East-Kalibhit-Forests-WRCS.pdf ; institutional long-term field technical report, printed pp.26–28 and 45–46; accessed 2026-11-05.
- **Scope / facts:** field work Dec 2012–Apr 2017. The report profiles banded females, males and juveniles with sex/status-labeled field photographs; p.26 identifies a sex-unknown Bandababa juvenile naturally perched beside a tree trunk. Fifteen nests were located in 2013–2017; the study area includes dry, open woody vegetation and farm-land edges.
- **Limits / design use:** the juvenile profile has no hatch date, exact age, iris chronology or feather sequence, so it cross-checks natural support/environment only and does not replace the BNHS known-stage sequence. Adult photos do not establish visible sex dimorphism.

### Re-read primary evidence and corrected boundary
- Re-read `FOW-MEHTA-RSD-2021`: 27 females and 19 males, ten measurements, none significantly different; adult display remains equal-scale/shared-plumage. The search result's PDF redirect was malformed, so the paper's indexed abstract and repository record were used, not unrelated PDF lines.
- Re-read `FOW-ISHTIAQ-2005`, pp.197–205: four nests, three intensively monitored; chicks fledged at about 40 d and remained parent-dependent about 45 d. This is retained as study/sample variation rather than a universal day.
- Re-read `FOW-JATHAR-2004`, breeding report pp.49–55: 28–30 d young emerged/perched nearby, darker with stumpy tail, ill-developed primaries/secondaries, highly blotched ventrum and weak reluctant flight.
- Reused `FOW-MIKKOLA-2013`, `FOW-KOPARDE-2018` and `FOW-KHAN-2023-HABITAT`; uploaded Mikkola was unavailable and not represented as freshly read.
- Correction: prior v4 prompt allowed a yellow juvenile iris despite the card listing iris chronology unknown. V5 uses a dark age-indeterminate iris and keeps the 28–30 d bird supported; adult-like alertness does not prove adult iris colour.
- Current name: *Athene blewitti*; historical *Heteroglaux blewitti*.
- Unknown: exact individual age, iris transition, daily feather chronology, population variation, universal nest geometry and any diagnostic visible adult sex character.
- Source accounting: new verified 1; re-read/reused verified 6; corrected boundaries 2.
- Links: `research/species/43-athene-blewitti-v5.md`; `production/runs/20261105T022100+0800-cycle-5.md`; planned concept v5.


## 2026-11-05 — #44 *Strix uralensis* v5 — day-29 mesoptile and early brancher boundary

### UROW-KAZAMA-JBBA-2005-P28-32
- **Title / author / year:** *Notes on Breeding Ecology of Ural Owl in Niigata Prefecture* — Kazama, 2005.
- **DOI / URL / type / pages / accessed:** https://doi.org/10.14491/jbba.AR135 ; https://www.jstage.jst.go.jp/article/jbba/19/1/19_28/_article ; primary nest-box field study, *Journal of the Japanese Bird Banding Association* 19(1):28–32; accessed 2026-11-05.
- **Scope / facts:** fifty nest boxes were monitored, twenty were used, and 43 chicks were banded. For roughly the first two weeks after exiting the nest, fledglings remained on branches within about 30 m and were provisioned by adults.
- **Limits / design use:** the paper supports natural branch support and continued dependence after nest exit, not exact day-29 plumage, first-flight strength, or a universal distance. The juvenile remains supported and dependent; no independent flight or hunting pose.

### UROW-ERIKSSON-1984-P313-316 — re-read primary growth series
- **Title / authors / year:** *Growth and survival of Ural Owl nestlings* — Eriksson, Lundberg & Westman, 1984.
- **URL / type / pages / accessed:** https://www.annzool.net/PDF/anzf21/anz21-313-316.pdf ; primary multi-nest growth study, *Annales Zoologici Fennici* 21:313–316; re-read 2026-11-05.
- **Facts:** eyes open around day 3 and feathers emerge around day 5. Wing length and fourth-primary sheath length predict age with about one-day mean error. Adult females are clearly larger, but size dimorphism is not visible until the end of the nestling period.
- **Limits / correction:** the printed graph was not used to invent an exact day-29 wing length. Adult female size is shown moderately; the unsexed juvenile is not sex-scaled.

### UROW-SCHERZINGER-1980-P45-56 — re-read captive development monograph
- **Title / author / year:** *Zur Ethologie und Jugendentwicklung der Habichtskäuze (Strix uralensis)* — Scherzinger, 1980.
- **URL / type / pages / accessed:** https://www.biodiversitylibrary.org/item/195813 ; captive-pair developmental monograph, pp.45–56; re-read 2026-11-05.
- **Scope / facts:** five captive pairs and 27 young; the mesoptile begins around day 5 and covers the body by about day 16; wing quill sheaths appear around day 10, while the tail begins much later (around day 28).
- **Limits / use:** captive chronology is a species-specific boundary but not a universal wild schedule. V5 shows a mesoptile-dominant day-29 bird with a short folded wing and little/hidden tail, not adult remiges.

### Reuse, mapping and correction
- Reused `UROW-MIKKOLA-2013`, `UROW-LOHMUS-2003`, and `UROW-BROMMER-2004` from the v4 record; the uploaded Mikkola file was unavailable and was not represented as freshly read.
- Current name: *Strix uralensis*; historical combinations in the repository mapping include *Syrnium uralense* / *Syrnium uralensis*.
- Corrected boundaries: branch departure does not establish independent flight, and female-larger applies to adults rather than an unsexed juvenile. Exact day-29 wing length, iris transition, first-flight performance, population colour variation and universal nest geometry remain unknown.
- Source accounting: new verified 1; re-read/reused verified 6; corrected boundaries 2.
- Links: `research/species/44-strix-uralensis-v5.md`; `production/runs/20261105T023100+0800-cycle-6.md`; planned concept v5.


## 2026-11-05 — #45 *Strix seloputo* v5 — dated brancher, balance-flap and first-flight boundary

### SOWL-BESG-2022-P1 — re-read dated family record
- **Title / author / year:** *Spotted wood owl (Strix seloputo) family at Pasir Ris Park, Singapore: Part 1* — LW Teo / Bird Ecology Study Group, 2022.
- **URL / type / sections / accessed:** https://besgroup.org/2022/02/27/spotted-wood-owl-strix-seloputo-family-at-pasir-ris-park-singapore-part-1/ ; dated field-photography chronology, paragraphs 19–27 and photo captions; re-read 2026-11-05.
- **Facts:** one pair nested in a bird's-nest fern about 5 m up a rain tree. Part 1 dates the first fledging to 18 February and the second to 21 February 2022. The owlets moved precariously along branches and flapped while walking to maintain balance; parents watched from nearby branches.
- **Limits / design use:** one family and one site. The fern/rain-tree setting is contextual, not universal nest architecture. Balance flapping is not independent flight.

### SOWL-BESG-2022-P2 — re-read growth and locomotion chronology
- **Title / author / year:** *Spotted wood owl (Strix seloputo) family at Pasir Ris Park, Singapore: Part 2* — LW Teo / Bird Ecology Study Group, 2022.
- **URL / type / sections / accessed:** https://besgroup.org/2022/04/01/spotted-wood-owl-strix-seloputo-family-at-pasir-ris-park-singapore-part-2/ ; dated follow-up field chronology, paragraphs 18–24 and captions/video notes; re-read 2026-11-05.
- **Facts:** on fledging the owlets were about half adult size; remiges lengthened over weeks while rectrices remained shorter. A 23 February video shows branch walking with wing flaps for balance. By 11 March they were observed flying branch-to-branch; by 24 March their patterns were adult-like while down remained visible on the head.
- **Conflict / correction:** Part 2 dates the first fledging to 19 February, one day later than Part 1. The design therefore uses the conservative label “2–4 days post-fledging in one documented family” for the 23 February observation and does not assert an exact hatch age.

### SOWL-BESG-2018-FALLEN-OWLET — new verified behavior boundary
- **Title / author / year:** *An owlet fell from its nest…* — YC Wee / Bird Ecology Study Group, 2018.
- **URL / type / sections / accessed:** https://besgroup.org/2018/05/04/an-owlet-fell-from-its-nest/ ; dated field observation and rescue note, paragraphs 27–42; accessed 2026-11-05.
- **Facts:** an age-unknown *S. seloputo* chick walked, flapped and scrambled up a trunk but was unable to fly; after losing grip it fell, and was moved to a stable raised surface.
- **Limits / design use:** no age or feather chronology. It supports the requirement for visible support and the inference boundary that flapping/scrambling does not prove flight.

### SOWL-NPARKS-FFW-2019 — re-read adult identification and current name
- **Institution / updated:** NParks Flora & Fauna Web; species record updated 21 August 2019, page re-read 2026-11-05.
- **URL / type / sections:** https://www.nparks.gov.sg/florafaunaweb/fauna/2/6/269 ; Singapore government species account, description and habitat.
- **Facts:** current name *Strix seloputo* Horsfield, 1821; earless head, bold black-barred buffy underparts, dark brown upperparts with white spots, dark brown eyes, grey bill and feathered tarsi; forest/woodland.
- **Limits:** adult account; no sex-size direction or juvenile developmental sequence.

### SOWL-MBR-OOI-2025-P39-40 — re-read age-unknown juvenile support evidence
- **Title / author / year:** *Crested Goshawk Attacking a Juvenile Spotted Wood-Owl* — Winnie Ooi, 2025.
- **URL / type / pages / accessed:** https://malaysianbird.report/wp-content/uploads/9-Juvenile-Spotted-Wood-Owl-Malaysian-Bird-Report-Sept-2025.pdf ; field note, Malaysian Bird Report 3/2025, pp.39–40; re-read 2026-11-05.
- **Facts:** an age-unknown juvenile was perched in a tree and nearly lost balance during an attack; both adults chased the goshawk.
- **Limits:** no hatch date, locomotor stage or plumage chronology; support/context only.

### V5 evidence accounting and mapping
- Reused indexed Mikkola printed pp.322–323 (PDF pp.324–325): adult sex-size difference unknown; pale fledgling with face/primaries beginning to resemble the adult while the back remains distinctive. The uploaded guide was unavailable in this execution and was not represented as freshly read.
- Current name: *Strix seloputo* Horsfield, 1821; no replacement old combination was introduced this cycle.
- New verified sources: 1; re-read/reused verified sources: 5; corrected evidence boundaries: 2.
- Corrected boundaries: (1) 18/19 February conflict is retained rather than silently resolved; (2) balance flapping and trunk scrambling are not independent flight.
- Unknown: exact hatch age, sex-specific growth, independent-flight threshold for the 23 February individuals, universal nest geometry and population-specific plumage variation.
- Links: `research/species/45-strix-seloputo-v5.md`; `production/runs/20261105T033723+0800-cycle-1.md`; planned concept v5.


## 2026-11-05 — #46 *Strix ocellata* v4 — day-34–36 branch-walk versus species-wide flight boundary

### MOWL-ELA-FACTFILE-2012 — new verified illustrated development reference
- **Title / institution / year:** *Fact File: Mottled Wood Owl Strix ocellata* — Ela Foundation, 2012.
- **URL / type / page / accessed:** https://www.elafoundation.org/ela/wp-content/uploads/2017/03/FACT-FILE_02_Mottled-wood-owl.pdf ; institutional illustrated fact sheet, single printed page; accessed 2026-11-05.
- **Facts:** shows separately captioned two-day hatchlings, two-week nestling, three-and-a-half-week chicks, and a four-week chick being fed in a hollow. Adults have coral-red/pink eyelid rims; Strix lacks ear tufts and usually has dark irides. Typical photographed setting is a top-opening natural cavity in an old mango tree in farmland; other large trees/groves occur.
- **Limits / design use:** captions are stage anchors, not a continuous daily feather sequence or quantified flight test. One typical cavity is contextual, not universal.

### MOWL-DESHPANDE-WOC-2022 — reused indexed primary serial biometry
- **Scope / facts:** repository-indexed field serial measurements support a day-34–36 nest-leaving bird moving from an open-top cavity to an adjacent branch by walking; tarsus and beak were about 90% of adult measures while wing and tail were about 50%.
- **Limits / correction:** the field sample does not establish a universal first-flight day. V4 encodes branch walking, compact wing/tail and continuous support, not launch or independent flight.

### MOWL-PANDE-JRR-2018 — re-read breeding population context
- **Title / authors / year:** *Breeding Biology of the Mottled Wood-Owl (Strix ocellata) in West-central India* — Pande, Yosef, Deshpande, Pawar & Mahajan, 2018.
- **DOI / URL / type / pages / accessed:** https://doi.org/10.3356/JRR-17-53.1 ; https://agris.fao.org/search/en/providers/122535/records/65dfb6c263b8185d9cae413d ; peer-reviewed nine-season field study, JRR 52(2):240–244; re-read 2026-11-05.
- **Facts:** 15 pairs were studied from 2005–2016; mango and tamarind were major nest trees; habitat was a mosaic of agricultural land, scrub/deciduous forest, grassland, habitation and waterways.
- **Limits:** aggregate breeding output and habitat; no day-specific plumage or flight threshold in the accessible record.

### MOWL-OWLPAGES-ID-2020 — re-read adult identification
- **URL / type / sections:** https://www.owlpages.com/owls/species.php?s=1530 ; secondary species account, description lines 50–80; re-read 2026-11-05.
- **Facts:** rounded untufted head; whitish facial disc with fine concentric dark bars; dark-brown eyes; dusky pink/coral eyelids; horn-black bill; densely mottled reddish-brown/black/white/buff upperparts and barred pale/orange-buff underparts.
- **Limits / correction:** “female likely larger” is not a quantified sex measurement and is not used as a visible size rule.

### MOWL-PEREGRINE-2026 — re-read explicit knowledge gap
- **URL / type / sections:** https://peregrinefund.org/explore-raptors-species/owls/mottled-wood-owl ; institutional overview, nests/young lines 52–57; re-read 2026-11-05.
- **Facts / use:** natural tree hollows/cracks are used; the institution explicitly states that the age at which young fly from the nest remains poorly known. This prevents treating a branch departure as species-wide independent flight.

### V4 evidence accounting
- Reused repository-indexed Mikkola and Deshpande 2018/Kumar 2020 records; uploaded Mikkola was unavailable and not represented as freshly read.
- Current name: *Strix ocellata* (Lesson, 1839); no replacement historical combination was introduced in this cycle.
- New verified sources: 1; re-read/reused verified sources: 5; corrected boundaries: 2.
- Unknown: daily plumage sequence, universal first-flight age, sex-size direction, iris/eyelid ontogeny, population transfer and universal cavity geometry.
- Links: `research/species/46-strix-ocellata-v4.md`; `production/runs/20261105T040500+0800-cycle-2.md`; planned concept v4.

## 2026-11-05 — #47 *Strix leptogrammica* v5 — Sri Lankan day-34 cavity-chick boundary

### BROW-SAMARAWICKRAMA-2006-P73-78
- **Title / authors / year:** *Observations on the nesting of the Brown Wood Owl, Strix leptogrammica ochrogenys in Gannoruwa Forest Reserve, Sri Lanka* — V.A.M.P.K. Samarawickrama, K.B. Ranawana & V.A.P. Samarawickrama, 2006.
- **URL / accessed:** https://citeseerx.ist.psu.edu/document?doi=0e0e59e777e7dd4edd5a36ae4d4ba1fe2aa34e85&repid=rep1&type=pdf ; accessed 2026-11-05.
- **Type / pages / scope:** primary four-year nest study; *Ceylon Journal of Science* 35(1), printed pp.73–78; Sri Lankan population labelled *S. l. ochrogenys*; adults, nest and chick development.
- **Key facts:** adults described with red-brown facial disc and wide dark border, brown-striped upperparts, white throat, pale chocolate-barred underparts and brown eyes; jackfruit-tree cavity about 10 m high, c.45 cm wide, 60 cm long and 90 cm deep; female-only incubation 25–30 d; young dependent nearly two months. Figure 2 separately labels 24-day, 34-day, ready-to-fly and nine-week stages.
- **Limits / conflict:** text does not give a day-34 remige measurement, independent-flight date or quantified sex-size ratio. The 2006 population label conflicts with later guide treatments and is preserved as source wording, not silently modernized.
- **Design decision:** select the explicitly labelled 34-day cavity chick; retain full cavity support, folded short wing and no flight claim. Exclude eggs/prey from the three-age plate.
- **Links:** `research/species/47-strix-leptogrammica-v5.md`; concept v5; run `production/runs/20261105T040706+0800-cycle-3.md`.

### BROW-KFBG-TAN-2011-P6-7
- **Title / authors / year:** *Post Release Monitoring of a Rehabilitated Brown Wood Owl in Hong Kong SAR, China* — Tan Kit Sun et al., Kadoorie Farm and Botanic Garden Publication Series 7, 2011.
- **URL / accessed:** https://www.kfbg.org/upload/Documents/Free-Resources-Download/Report-and-Document/BrownWoodOwlReport_2011Nov.pdf ; accessed 2026-11-05.
- **Type / pages / scope:** institutional rehabilitation and post-release report; printed pp.6–7, Figure 4; Hong Kong nestling, fledgling and immature stages.
- **Key facts:** Figure 4 distinguishes nestling (16 Apr 2009), fledgling (21 May 2009, including wing view) and immature (May 2010) plumages; release assessment later required good flight and prey capture.
- **Limits / conflict:** rescued bird age and Hong Kong subspecies were not securely known; later-stage plumage and locomotion cannot be transferred to the Sri Lankan day-34 source population.
- **Design decision:** use only as a conflict gate: do not give the selected Sri Lankan cavity chick the later Hong Kong fledgling's adult-type wing/tail or flight posture.

### BROW-ITIS-555427-2026
- **Institution / accessed:** Integrated Taxonomic Information System; accessed 2026-11-05.
- **URL:** https://www.itis.gov/servlet/SingleRpt/SingleRpt?print_version=PRT&search_topic=TSN&search_value=555427&source=to_print
- **Type / scope:** official taxonomy; species and subspecies name mapping.
- **Facts:** *Strix leptogrammica* Temminck, 1832 is valid; listed direct children include *S. l. ochrogenys* and *S. l. indranee* as separate subspecies.
- **Limits:** no morphology or development; does not resolve how the 2006 Sri Lankan population label maps to every later checklist.
- **Design decision:** retain repository canonical species name and preserve the paper's source-population label in research metadata, not on the artwork title.

### Reuse and correction
- Reused `BROW-MIKKOLA-2013-P326-327` from the existing #47 record; the user-uploaded file was unavailable and was not freshly read.
- Corrected boundary: the paper's “34-day chick” and separately captioned “ready to fly” stage must not be collapsed. Adult mass/incubation direction does not justify a large display-height difference.


## 2026-11-05 — #48 *Strix nivicolum* v5 — Taiwanese juvenile and taxonomy boundary

### HIML-NTM-YAMADAE
- **Title / institution / record:** 灰林鴞 collection record TMAV0249 — National Taiwan Museum.
- **URL / type / section / accessed:** https://collections.culture.tw/ntm_collectionsweb/Collection?GID=0WMRM60WMB ; official museum collection/species record, lines 73–113; accessed 2026-11-05.
- **Scope / facts:** *Strix nivicolum yamadae*; round untufted head, yellow-green bill, buff/brown feathered tarsi/toes, dark-edged tawny/brown facial disc, dark back, cream scapular patches, buff-barred wing/tail and pale crossed underparts. Juvenile is described as very pale with sparse white down on head/underparts and blurred dark ventral marks that darken/sharpen with age.
- **Limits:** TMAV0249 has damaged wing and tail and no juvenile age or locomotor data; prose may synthesize the taxon rather than one juvenile specimen. Supports palette/identity and an age-unknown stage only.
- **Links:** `research/species/48-strix-nivicolum-v5.md`; concept v5; run `owl-pet/production/runs/20261105T043300+0800-cycle-4.md`.

### HIML-LEE-2025
- **Title / author / year:** *Nest Box Utilization by the Himalayan Owl (Strix nivicolum) and Its Implications for Conservation Planning in South Korea* — Sang-Yeon Lee, 2025.

### RLO-VUKASOVIC-2006-P172-174
- **Title / authors / year:** *Nesting Record of Rufous-legged Owl (Strix rufipes King) in Central Chile* — M. Angélica Vukasovic, Martín A. H. Escobar, Jorge A. Tomasevic & Cristián F. Estades, 2006.
- **DOI / URL:** https://doi.org/10.3356/0892-1016(2006)40[172:NROROS]2.0.CO;2 ; https://levs.cl/wp-content/uploads/2023/07/25.-Vukasovic-M.-A.-M.-A.-H.-Escobar-J.-A.-Tomasevic-C.F.-Estades.-2006.-Nesting-Record-of-Rufous-legged-Owl-Strix-rufipes-King-in-central-Chile.pdf
- **Type / pages / accessed:** primary field paper, *Journal of Raptor Research* 40(2):172–174; full article read 2026-11-05.
- **Scope / facts:** one central-Chile owlet in a roughly 30 cm top-opening cup cavity formed by decay in a dead broken *Nothofagus glauca* trunk, 3 m above ground in second-growth hualo forest; estimated at no more than one week using other *Strix* growth patterns; brooded by day, fed at night and observed pecking cavity walls.
- **Limits / conflict:** age was inferred, not measured from hatching; the paper does not describe iris, feather sequence or flight. One nest must not become a universal cavity geometry.
- **Design use:** estimated ≤1-week, continuously supported, down-dominant cavity chick and gentle wall-pecking action; no flight, eggs or stored prey.

## 2026-11-05 — #59 *Ketupa zeylonensis* v5 — Hong Kong brancher and full-resolution boundary

### BFOW-HKBWS-AVIFAUNA-2026
- **Title / institution / accessed:** Brown Fish Owl species account — *The Avifauna of Hong Kong*, Hong Kong Bird Watching Society; accessed 2026-11-05.
- **URL / type / sections:** https://avifauna.hkbws.org.hk/species/0190/027400 ; institutional regional avifauna; Identification, Distribution & Habitat Preference and Breeding sections; no page numbers.
- **Species/stage/facts:** *Ketupa zeylonensis* adults and Hong Kong young. Adults 48–55 cm with loose ear tufts, usually yellow eyes, grey bill, long unfeathered legs/pale-yellow feet, white throat and heavily streaked/crossbarred plumage. Some juveniles are paler on mantle/coverts/scapulars, with narrower/sparser breast streaks and smaller tufts. Seven nest sites included roofs, ledges, buttress roots and a natural hollow; date ranges distinguish downy chicks, unfledged juveniles, first flight and post-fledging dependency.
- **Limits/conflicts/credibility:** institutional synthesis from regional records, not a known-age feather series; dates overlap, light angle alters apparent iris colour and no sexed measurements are supplied. High value for Hong Kong identity/habitat/stage boundaries; not an exact-age or universal-nest source.
- **Design/check decision:** bare tarsi, local waterside woodland, paler smaller-tuft juvenile and nest-form diversity; no exact age, universal nest or adult size multiplier.
- **Links:** `research/species/59-ketupa-zeylonensis-v5.md`; planned concept v5; run `production/runs/20261105T103554+0800-cycle-1.md`.

### BFOW-KFBG-2021
- **Title / author / institution / year:** “What secrets can we discover at a Brown Fish Owl’s nesting site?” — Aiko Leong, Kadoorie Farm and Botanic Garden, 2021.
- **URL / type / sections:** https://www.kfbg.org/en/KFBG-blog/post/What-secrets-can-we-discover-at-a-Brown-Fish-Owls-nesting-site ; institutional field/photo account, article lines 45–64 and two juvenile captions; no page numbers; re-read 2026-11-05.
- **Facts:** one New Territories pair reared one young; the well-feathered nestling was photographed shortly before nest departure, later mobile and exploring nearby branches; wood/bark material occurred at the nest site.
- **Limits/design:** no hatch date, sex, feather measurements or flight test; one site does not define all nests. Supports an age-unknown, bilaterally supported just-mobile brancher beside a tree nest, not flight.

### BFO-INDIANBIRDS-2013-P147-151
- **Title / authors / year:** “Notes on the breeding of the Brown Fish Owl *Ketupa zeylonensis*” — Raju Vyas, Kartik Upadhayay, Mital R. Patel, Rahul D. Bhatt & Pritesh Patel, 2013.
- **URL / type / pages:** https://indianbirds.in/pdfs/IB_8.6_HighRes.pdf ; primary breeding observations, *Indian BIRDS* 8(6):147–151; full article re-read 2026-11-05.
- **Facts:** active fluffy-faced 15–20-day chicks had smaller body/wing feathers; ear tufts developed later. Near departure the two young were almost adult-sized, stayed 4–6 days in the same tree canopy and remained parent-dependent. Paired adults could sometimes be separated by size when together.
- **Limits/design:** Gujarat sequence does not date a Hong Kong individual; no universal sex ratio or controlled flight threshold. Supports developed-but-immature folded wing/tail and only restrained adult fullness difference.

### BFOW-BINDU-BALAKRISHNAN-2015-P97-98
- **Title / authors / year:** “Observations on the Breeding of the Brown Fish-Owl *Ketupa zeylonensis* in Kerala, Southern India” — T. N. Bindu & Peroth Balakrishnan, 2015.
- **DOI / URL / type / pages:** https://doi.org/10.17087/jbnhs/2015/v112i2/104938 ; primary nine-year field note, *JBNHS* 112(2):97–98; full text re-read 2026-11-05.
- **Facts:** nine attempts reused a mango-tree crevice 5.2 m high within 30 m of a pond; nestling period 43–49 days, mean 46±3, n=3.
- **Limits/design:** small Kerala sample, one reused site, no day-by-day feather plate. Broad developmental context only; no exact Hong Kong age or universal nest form.

### V5 accounting and unknowns
- New persistent source records: 4 (one new institutional source; three restored/re-read records previously referenced by v4 but absent from the compact index); reused4; corrected boundaries3.
- Corrections: (1) nest departure/branch movement does not prove sustained flight; (2) paired-size observation is not a universal height ratio; (3) Kerala/Gujarat timing does not assign a Hong Kong individual an exact age.
- Current name *Ketupa zeylonensis*; historical/original combination *Strix zeylonensis*. Uploaded Mikkola guide unavailable; no fresh page claim.
- Unknown: exact juvenile age/sex/subspecies, continuous feather chronology, first-flight competence, quantified sex dimensions and cross-population transfer. Scientific status remains false.

- **Re-read:** `BFOW-NPARKS-BIODIVERSITYSG-2026`, `BFOW-BESG-NEST-2016`, `BFOW-SBG-BREED-2016`; **reused:** `BFOW-BESG-FLEDGE-2016`; new 0, corrected boundary 1.

BESG lines 11–47 directly support a down-covered, mostly resting/occasionally shifting chick in living *Asplenium nidus*. Singapore Bird Group lines 11–32 state that 25 March was about two weeks after **discovery**, not a known hatch date; the later branch bird and inferred short flight are not transferred backward.

## 2026-11-05 — #63 *Scotopelia peli* v5 — large downy river-roost juvenile boundary

### PFOW-SANPARKS-KRUGER-2007-P6
- **Title / author / institution / year:** *Kruger National Park — Rare Bird Sightings 2007*, Pafuri report by Martin Benadie — South African National Parks, 2007.
- **URL / page / accessed:** https://www.sanparks.org/wp-content/uploads/2021/03/2007-kruger.pdf ; PDF p.6, “Pafuri Area — 3 to 6 June 2007”; full page and text read 2026-11-05.
- **Type / scope:** first-hand institutional field report; one adult with one “large downy chick” along the Luvuvhu east, plus another adult on the Luvuvhu west.
- **Facts:** the adult and large downy chick were found roosting along the river in riparian woodland. The report independently anchors a large, still-downy juvenile outside a fabricated detailed hatchling sequence.
- **Limits / conflicts / credibility:** no hatch date, sex, dimensions, feather-tract chronology, exact perch description or flight test; a single June observation cannot define all populations. High credibility for occurrence, stage wording and riverine context; insufficient for exact age/action.
- **Design decision:** depict an age-unknown large downy juvenile low and fully supported on a broad riverine tree fork, with only compact down-softened folded wing contours and no flight pose. The fork is a conservative support synthesis, not a claimed photographed nest or universal roost.
- **Links:** `research/species/63-scotopelia-peli-v5.md`; planned concept v5; run `production/runs/20261105T133651+0800-cycle-5.md`.

### Reused and corrected boundaries
- Re-read `PFOW-BIRDLIFE-SA-2025-ECOLOGY` and `PFOW-SANBI-03164-2026`: adults are similar; male deeper rufous, female only slightly larger; dark eyes, no distinct facial disc, bare powerful tarsi/feet; juveniles retain much pale white down through the first six months. Reused `PFO-BROOKE-1984-P128`: riparian forest over clear pools, ten-week nestling period and prolonged family association.
- Current name: *Scotopelia peli* (Bonaparte, 1850); basionym/old mapping *Strix peli* Bonaparte, 1850; historical *Bubo peli* references map to the same species.
- New verified sources: 1; re-read verified sources: 3; corrected boundaries: 3. “Large downy chick” is not an exact age; roosting does not prove independent flight; “female slightly larger” is not a fixed height multiplier.
- Uploaded Mikkola guide was unavailable in this run; no fresh page claim. Unknown: exact juvenile age/sex, continuous feather chronology, locomotor ability, perch geometry, universal sex-size ratio and cross-population transfer. Scientific status remains false.

# 2026-11-05 — #66 *Tyto capensis* v5 — sex overlap and face-colour conflict

### SEO-OSTRICH-RSD-2008
- **Title / authors / year:** *Sexual dimorphism of four owl species in South Africa* — Ansara-Ross, Wepener, Mundy & Wepener, 2008.
- **URL / DOI:** https://doi.org/10.2989/OSTRICH.2008.79.1.11.366
- **Type / accessed:** peer-reviewed primary morphometric study; author copy Methods, Results and Tables 1–2 re-read 2026-11-05.
- **Species/stage:** adult *Tyto capensis*; nine males and nine females among road-killed intact carcases collected in Gauteng in 2002–2003.
- **Facts:** male/female mean mass 412.59/469.05 g and mean body length 390.0/416.7 mm; significant p=.003/.048; the reported ranges overlap.
- **Limits/conflict:** one regional dead-carcase sample; it does not supply a fixed standing-height ratio, universal range-wide rule, or plumage sex key. Female mean mass must not be rendered as categorical height.
- **Design use:** modest female fullness/length only, close crown height, no universal “female larger” template.

## 2026-11-05 — #68 *Tyto tenebricosa* v5 — six-week cavity stage and variable apparent pair size

### GSO-HYEM-1979-P20-22
- **Title / author / year:** “Observations on Owls in the Upper Manning River District, N.S.W.” — E. L. Hyem, 1979.
- **URL / type / pages / accessed:** https://absa.asn.au/wp-content/uploads/2021/07/Corella-3-2-17-25.pdf ; primary field observations, *Corella* 3(2):17–25; pp.20–22 read 2026-11-05.
- **Species/stage/facts:** *Tyto tenebricosa* adults and nestlings. Two young in one hollow were estimated at about six weeks and clothed in sooty-grey down; a separately described chick had well-defined facial discs and wing/tail feathers showing through the sooty-grey down. Recorded hollows ranged from about 40 cm to 5 m deep; one Blue Gum hollow was over 3 m deep and about 22 m high. Direct pair observations varied: one female appeared smaller, other pairs showed no visible difference, and at least two females appeared larger.
- **Limits/conflicts/credibility:** first-hand primary observations but not a controlled known-hatch-age feather series or morphometric study; the two chick descriptions may not be the same individual. Visual pair judgments conflict with a universal apparent female-larger rule and do not negate measured reversed sexual dimorphism. Individual hollow examples are not universal dimensions.
- **Design/check decision:** show an approximately-six-week, fully supported cavity chick in sooty-grey down with defined face and only emerging short wing/tail feathers; no flight or branch claim. Adults have comparable apparent crown height with only cautious fullness variation. Use a large roomy hollow without copying one exact geometry.
- **Links:** `research/species/68-tyto-tenebricosa-v5.md`; planned concept v5; run `production/runs/20261105T170300+0800-cycle-4.md`.

## 2026-11-05 — #69 *Tyto novaehollandiae* v5 — one-individual sequence and inferred-age gate

### AMO-DEBUS-WOOD-2007-P49-52
- **Title / authors / year:** “Growth of a Captive Nestling Masked Owl *Tyto novaehollandiae*” — S. J. S. Debus & C. Wood, 2007.
- **URL / type / pages / accessed:** https://afo.birdlife.org.au/afo/index.php/afo/article/viewFile/610/596 ; primary captive developmental sequence, *Australian Field Ornithology* 24:49–53; pp.49–52 and Plates 11–12 read 2026-11-05.
- **Species/stage/facts:** one female dark-morph nominate chick followed from mesoptile down to fledging. Upper Plate 11 was inferred around four weeks: downy with developing mask. Lower Plate 11 was inferred around five weeks: thick down, well-developed mask, no feathers visible through down. At inferred approximately eleven weeks the same fully grown fledgling was darker/heavier marked than its parents and retained traces of down on head, facial ruff and underparts.
- **Limits/conflicts/credibility:** precise laying/hatching dates were absent; ages were inferred by comparison; one captive individual had unlimited food. Strong within-individual stage evidence, but not a wild population growth curve or universal morph/subspecies chronology.
- **Design/check decision:** v5 uses only the earlier inferred approximately-four-week stage, with buff/ochre woolly down, developing mask, hidden wings/tail and no visible feather vanes; label retains “inferred.” V4 five-week and v3 fledgling traits are not transferred backward.
- **Links:** `research/species/69-tyto-novaehollandiae-v5.md`; planned concept v5; run `production/runs/20261105T171900+0800-cycle-5.md`.

## 2026-11-05 — #70 *Ninox strenua* v5 — photographic sex boundary and first-flight restraint

### POWERFUL-COOKE-ETAL-2020
- **Title / authors / year:** “Photography can determine the sex of a predator with limited sexual dimorphism: A case study of the powerful owl” — Cooke, Rendall, Weston, Porch, Bradsworth & White, 2020.
- **DOI / URL:** https://doi.org/10.1016/j.gecco.2020.e00959 ; https://www.researchgate.net/publication/339096218_Photography_can_determine_the_sex_of_a_predator_with_limited_sexual_dimorphism_A_case_study_of_the_powerful_owl
- **Type / pages / accessed:** open-access peer-reviewed primary photographic-morphometric study, *Global Ecology and Conservation* 22:e00959, pp.1–8, especially pp.2–6 and Figs.1–6; full article text read 2026-11-05.
- **Species/stage/facts:** adult *Ninox strenua*. Sixty-three known-sex front-facing alert photographs supplied the measured model; the head-width/inter-nostril ratio was larger in males. In 82 assessable images, female facemask feathers reached or slightly exceeded the horizontal head contour whereas male facemask feathers stopped short. Overall sexual dimorphism was described as extremely limited.
- **Limits/conflicts/credibility:** the method requires direct forward alert posture and image ratios; posture, angle and feather fluff distort measurement, and the male mask-to-contour gap can be small. Strong primary evidence for a restrained front-view diagnostic, not a fixed stylised pixel ratio or conspicuous height difference.
- **Design/check decision:** keep adult crown/support height comparable; use only a subtly broader male head/body and a female mask edge reaching the head contour. Do not exaggerate scale or invent dichromatism.
- **Links:** `research/species/70-ninox-strenua-v5.md`; planned concept v5; run `production/runs/20261105T173620+0800-cycle-6.md`.

### Re-read, reused and corrected boundaries
- Re-read `POWERFUL-MO-WATERHOUSE-2015` pp.145–146/Fig.1: two owlets left on 5/14 August, the second flew out then crashed, and both were first seen flying on 17 August. A recent fledgling can have developed folded wings/tail but must not be shown as universally competent in flight.
- Re-read `POWERFUL-ABSA-2019` p.1 and `POWERFUL-NSWSC-2008` pp.1–2; reused `POWERFUL-MV-2026` and repository extraction `WHSO-MIKKOLA-2013` printed pp.450–451/PDF pp.452–453.
- New1 / re-read3 / reused2 / corrected3: sex-scale becomes near-equal with a subtle facial diagnostic; leaving a hollow is not polished flight competence; the recent fledgling keeps white/downier juvenile plumage rather than adult chevrons.
- Uploaded Mikkola guide unavailable in this execution; no fresh page claim. Unknown: exact hatch age, depicted juvenile sex, daily feather chronology, universal first-flight competence and stylised transfer of photograph ratios. Scientific status remains false.

Young were clumsy on the first days, stronger by Day 14; one Day-30 juvenile manipulated paperbark between bill and foot; by Day 33 wing/tail proportions were adult-like but collar and thighs remained downy and rectrices retained white tips; adult-like ventral plumage emerged around Day 41.

# MOR-STEPHENSON-THESIS-1998

- Title: *The ecology and breeding biology of morepork, Ninox novaeseelandiae, and their risk from secondary poisoning, in New Zealand*
- Author/institution/year: Brent Mark Stephenson; Massey University; 1998
- URL: http://hdl.handle.net/10179/9851 ; full PDF https://mro.massey.ac.nz/server/api/core/bitstreams/a71313ab-b6a0-46b5-84fa-a07309da4b89/content
- Accessed: 2026-11-05
- Type: primary MSc thesis / field capture, telemetry and breeding study
- Taxon/stage: New Zealand *Ninox novaeseelandiae*; adults and immatures; Mokoia Island population
- Pages/sections read: abstract; printed pp.30–33 (plumage, immatures, moult, sex morphology); chapter map for printed pp.91–115
- Source fact: external plumage/morphometrics usually could not sex captured birds; known pairs looked alike in the field; immatures had a less distinct disc but broadly similar plumage. Captured females had significantly longer wing/bill width and higher mass.
- Design support: near-equal displayed adult height with only restrained female fullness/wing direction; softer juvenile facial disc. Does not support a fixed visible sex ratio or day-by-day feather geometry.
- Limits/confidence: strong primary evidence for the studied Mokoia sample; population transfer and exact visual ratios remain unknown. Linked to `research/species/74-ninox-novaeseelandiae-v5.md`, v5 image/review and batch cycle 4.

## 2026-11-05 — #75 *Ninox scutulata* v5 — photographed Singapore active-fledgling boundary

### SCU-SGRAPTOR-2024-P2
- **Title / authorship / year:** *Singapore Raptor Report: Late Spring Migration, April–June 2024* — Singapore Bird Group, 2024.
- **URL / page / accessed:** https://singaporebirdgroup.wordpress.com/wp-content/uploads/2024/08/singapore-raptor-report-late-spring-migration-apr-jun-2024.pdf ; printed/PDF p.2, lines 26–28; full relevant page read 2026-11-05.
- **Type / species / stage:** regional field-report compilation; *Ninox scutulata* family at Windsor Nature Park, 7 June 2024.
- **Facts:** the report records one family with two adults and two chicks at Windsor. This independently fixes family composition, locality and post-nest family context near the dated photographs.
- **Limits / conflict:** “chicks” is a report category, not a known hatch age or plumage description; the report supplies no sex, measurements, flight test or feather sequence.
- **Design/check decision:** use a Singapore dependent-young context and exactly one juvenile in the comparison plate, but do not infer age or juvenile sex. The photographs below, not this wording alone, control wing/tail maturity.

### SCU-BESG-2024-PHOTOS
- **Title / author / year:** “Brown Hawk Owl juveniles” — Andy Chew / Bird Ecology Study Group, 14 June 2024.
- **URL / figures / accessed:** https://besgroup.org/2024/06/14/brown-hawk-owl-juveniles/ ; text lines 19–32 and gallery images 224–227 actually opened 2026-11-05; no printed page.
- **Type / species / stage:** dated Singapore field observation and photographic series; two dependent post-fledging juveniles at Windsor Nature Park, 13 June 2024.
- **Facts:** four birds were high in the canopy; the two juveniles disturbed a parent and performed multiple “stunts.” Opened photographs show pale down retained over crown, face and much of the underbody while long, fully formed brown wing feathers and a developed barred tail are present. One juvenile stretches a formed wing laterally and braces on a fork; another leans head-down while gripping a branch.
- **Limits / conflict:** exact age, sex, individual identity across frames, flight competence and daily feather chronology are absent. The gallery is one family and cannot define all Singapore young. Existing v4 underrepresented the photographed formed wing/tail by treating them as merely short growing feathers.
- **Design/check decision:** v5 selects the photographed age-unknown active dependent stage: formed but juvenile-irregular wing/tail, residual pale down, one restrained wing-stretch, and clear bilateral branch support. Do not regress this stage into a short-wing hatchling or polish it into an adult-patterned miniature.

### SCU-GIBSONHILL-1949-P103-104
- **Title / author / year:** *An Annotated Checklist of the Birds of Malaya* — C. A. Gibson-Hill, 1949.
- **URL / pages / accessed:** https://lkcnhm.nus.edu.sg/app/uploads/2017/04/20brm005-299.pdf ; printed pp.103–104 / extracted lines 2559–2575 read 2026-11-05.
- **Type / taxonomy:** historical Raffles Museum checklist; *Ninox scutulata* complex in Malaya and Singapore.
- **Facts:** it maps the original combination *Strix scutulata* and historical names *Ninox burmanica* and *Athene malaccensis*; it lists *N. s. malaccensis* as the resident lowland form occurring on Singapore.
- **Limits / conflict:** 1949 taxonomy predates modern Brown/Northern/Chocolate Boobook splits and cannot settle the current subspecies of the photographed family. The historical name/locality mapping is nomenclatural, not a license to borrow Japanese developmental criteria.
- **Design/check decision:** retain current repository name *Ninox scutulata*, record *Athene malaccensis* / *N. s. malaccensis* as historical Singapore mapping, and continue to exclude Japanese *japonica* age/rectrix evidence from direct design transfer.

### Re-read, reused and corrected boundaries
- Re-read `SCU-BESG-2024`, `SCU-NPARKS-2025`, `SCU-ITIS-2025`, `SCU-OWLPAGES-2021` and repository extraction `WHSO-MIKKOLA-2013` printed pp.464–465/PDF pp.466–467. The uploaded guide itself was not mounted in this run, so no fresh page transcription is claimed.

V5 retains a formed wing and barred tail with residual down and an active but fully supported pose. Conflicting unquantified adult sex-size directions remain unresolved, so displayed adults stay near-equal. Exact age, juvenile sex, flight threshold, current Singapore subspecies and continuous feather chronology remain unknown; scientific status false.

## 2026-11-05 — #76 *Asio clamator* v5 — dated pre-flight development and supported wing exercise

### CLA-AGUIAR-NAIFF-2009-L50-54
- **Title / authors / year:** “Aspectos reprodutivos e dieta alimentar dos ninhegos de *Rhinoptynx clamator*” — Kurazo Mateus Okada Aguiar & Rafael Homobono Naiff, 2009, *Acta Amazonica* 39(1).
- **URL / section / accessed:** https://acta.inpa.gov.br/fasciculos/39-1/BODY/v39n1a24.html ; Results lines 44–64 and Discussion lines 71–74 read 2026-11-05; HTML has no printed-page locator.
- **Type / species / stage:** primary field study with daily observations, dated hatch events and chick measurements; Amapá, Brazil; old combination *Rhinoptynx clamator* = *Asio clamator*.
- **Facts:** hatchlings had closed eyes/fine white down; remex/rectrix sheaths and facial disc appeared at day 13; young left the nest at dusk and exercised wings at day 20; head tufts appeared day 25; sleeping outside began day 26; approximately one-month young were well feathered but still unable to fly; first low flight for the older chick occurred later, summarized as day 38. The nest was on the ground at a palm base with sparse grass/twigs.
- **Limits / confidence:** one brood in Amapá; table formatting has an apparent wing-length transcription anomaly, so no numeric wing length is used. Timing is not transferred as a universal clock and does not establish sex or subspecies differences.
- **Design/check decision:** v5 may use a supported wing-exercise pose for an approximately 25–30-day dependent young with emerging remiges/tufts, but must keep it low, two-foot/abdomen supported and explicitly pre-flight. Adult-perfect spot rows remain unsupported.

### Re-read/reused and corrected boundary
- Re-read `CLA-PONCE-GONZALEZ-2025`, `CLA-PAUTASSO-DE-LA-PENA-2001`; reused `CLA-FORMOSO-ETAL-2025` and repository extraction `WHSO-MIKKOLA-2013`. New1 / re-read4 / corrected2.
- V4's static center bird and crisp repeated wing spots are not required by the selected 25–30-day stage. V5 retains brown down/emerging disc/tufts, adds evidence-supported low wing exercise and softens the growing wing to non-adult pattern. Exact universal chronology, juvenile sex, current subspecies transfer and adult sex-size direction remain unknown; science false.

## 2026-11-05 — #77 *Asio stygius* v5 — older branch-active juvenile boundary

### STY-CADENA-ORTIZ-2018-P23-25-F2-T3
- **Title / authors / year:** “Notas sobre la dieta, desarrollo de jóvenes y distribución del Búho Estigio *Asio stygius* (Strigiformes: Strigidae) en Ecuador” — Héctor Cadena-Ortiz, Jorge Bedoya, Glenda M. Pozo-Zamora, Julie Watson & Jorge Brito, 2018.
- **URL / DOI / pages / accessed:** https://revistas.usfq.edu.ec/index.php/reo/article/download/773/1244 ; https://doi.org/10.18272/reo.v0i3.773 ; printed pp.23–25, Table 3 and Figure 2; direct journal PDF text opened and printed-p.23 figure page rendered 2026-11-05.
- **Type / species / stage:** primary repeated field observations and photo sequence; two *Asio stygius* juveniles at Nayón, Pichincha, Ecuador; branch-active older juveniles inferred by authors as older than one month.
- **Facts:** the young retained scattered body down, black facial discs, small ear tufts, yellow irides, barred bellies and pale hind-crown feathers; later belly plumage darkened and early adult-like parallel/herringbone chest lines appeared. On 20 May they moved rapidly among branches in one tree and one extended its wings, interpreted by the observers as intimidation.
- **Limits / conflicts / confidence:** age is inferred, not hatch-dated; n=2 at one locality; no continuous measured feather chronology, flight threshold or juvenile sex. Do not transfer the branch behavior to the 26–27-day Brazilian ground stage.
- **Design/check decision:** select an “OLDER JUVENILE” with residual down and formed but non-adult wing/tail, two-foot branch grip and one low sideways wing extension. No exact age label, takeoff or flight claim. Adults stay near-equal because this source does not quantify sex size.
- **Links:** `research/species/77-asio-stygius-v5.md`; planned concept v5; run `production/runs/20261105T233209+0800-cycle-1.md`.

- **URL / pages / accessed:** https://sora.unm.edu/sites/default/files/journals/on/v015n03/p0423-p0428.pdf ; printed pp.423–425 and Fig.1; direct SORA PDF text opened 2026-11-05.
- **Type / species / stage:** primary hatch-dated nest monitoring; central Brazilian cerrado; 10-day Fig.1 and 26–27-day final ground-nestling observation.
- **Facts:** 17 cm × 3 cm shallow ground depression below a shrub under 70 cm; the 26–27-day young were slightly over two-thirds adult body size, with poorly developed remiges/rectrices that entirely prevented flight attempts.
- **Limits / conflicts / confidence:** one brood; final stage not a measured continuous plumage series; does not support branch movement. It directly constrains v4 and corrects any attempt to animate that same stage.
- **Design/check decision:** preserve v4 as the dated ground-stage concept; for v5 action, explicitly change to the older Ecuador stage rather than invent movement for this non-flying stage.
- **Links:** `research/species/77-asio-stygius-v4.md`; `research/species/77-asio-stygius-v5.md`; v4 and planned v5.

## 2026-11-06 — #81 *Otus scops* v5 — overlapping adult morphometrics and recent-fledgling boundary

### SCOPS-BOANO-ETAL-2020

- **Title / authors / year:** “Sex identification of Eurasian Scops Owl *Otus scops* using morphometric analysis” — G. Boano et al., 2020, *Ringing & Migration* 34(1):45–51.
- **URL / DOI / accessed:** https://doi.org/10.1080/03078698.2019.1759914 ; publisher full PDF read 2026-11-06; Methods, Results, Table 1 and Discussion.
- **Type / species / stages:** primary morphometric study; 105 genetically sexed *Otus scops*, including 33 juveniles and 68 adults.
- **Facts:** juvenile biometric sex differences were not significant. Adult females averaged longer wing (161.9 vs 156.4 mm), eighth primary (119.1 vs 115.5 mm), tail (71.4 vs 68.8 mm) and higher mass (92.0 vs 79.2 g), but ranges overlapped; bill and tarsus were not significantly different. The study provides no colour/plumage sex cue.
- **Limits / confidence:** strong sample-specific morphometric evidence, but region/season and overlap prevent a fixed visible standing-height ratio or individual sex assignment from the plate alone.
- **Design decision:** keep adult crown heights close; allow only a restrained female fullness and folded-wing/tail direction. Do not map colour morph to sex.
- **Links:** `research/species/81-otus-scops-v5.md`; v5 concept/review; `production/runs/20261106T025000+0800-cycle-5.md`.

## 2026-11-06 — #82 *Otus senegalensis* v5 — documented day-22 nest-box stage

### SEN-RIEKERT-1986-P425-428

- **Title / author / year:** “Some observations on the breeding biology of the African Scops Owl” — B. R. Riekert, 1986, *Madoqua* 14(4):425–428.
- **URL / pages / accessed:** https://the-eis.com/elibrary/sites/default/files/downloads/literature/Riekert_1986_Madoqua_14_4_African_Scops_Owl.pdf ; complete four-page PDF and page images read 2026-11-06; printed pp.425–428, especially pp.425–426.
- **Type / species / stage:** primary nest-box field study near Windhoek; one measured adult pair and several broods, with daily development notes.
- **Facts:** the measured pair had equal 140 mm wing chords while the female weighed 110 g and male 70 g; plumage was described as almost identical (p.425). Wing feathers emerged from sheaths at about day 14; by day 22 body feathers, including the tail, had largely emerged, and young called and scratched at the nest-box entrance; one brood left at day 23 (p.426). Nest boxes were used because cavities were limited in the arid woodland.
- **Limits / conflicts:** one adult pair cannot establish a universal mass or visible-size ratio. The study text does not provide a continuous photographic feather atlas, individual sex, or sustained-flight threshold. A later two-pair study reported a 25–28-day nestling period, so departure age varies and is not transferred to every bird.
- **Design decision:** depict a clearly labelled day-22 **study stage** on a broad nest-box sill, both feet supported, calling/scratching at the entrance; residual down plus largely emerged body/tail feathers, compact non-adult wing and no flight. Keep adult wing length/height equal; female may be subtly fuller in body mass but not taller.
- **Links:** `research/species/82-otus-senegalensis-v5.md`; planned v5 concept/review; run `production/runs/20261106T034111+0800-cycle-6.md`.

- **Facts/use:** *Scops senegalensis* Swainson, 1837 is the invalid original combination; valid name is *Otus senegalensis* (Swainson, 1837). Taxonomy only; no morphology or development inference.

### SEN-MELO-ETAL-2022

- **Title / authors / year:** “A new species of scops-owl … from Príncipe Island and novel insights into the systematic affinities within *Otus*” — Melo et al., 2022, *ZooKeys* 1126:1–54.

- **Limits / design use:** unsexed/uneven museum specimens cannot calibrate adult sex difference or juvenile growth. Use only for current mainland taxon scope and broad adult variation.

### ELE-SAWADA-IWASAKI-ETAL-2021-RSD
- **Title/authors/year:** “Reversed sexual size dimorphism in the Ryukyu Scops Owl *Otus elegans* on Minami-daito Island” — Sawada, Iwasaki, Matsuo, Akatani & Takagi, 2021, *Ornithological Science* 20:15–26.
- **URL/DOI/accessed:** https://www.jstage.jst.go.jp/article/osj/20/1/20_15/_pdf ; https://doi.org/10.2326/osj.20.15 ; full PDF read 2026-11-06.
- **Pages/species/stages:** printed pp.15–18; 770 *O. e. interpositus* individuals across 17 years; adults plus breeding-cycle context.
- **Facts:** females larger in mass, culmen, bill depth/width, tail and flattened wing; males larger in tarsus/head; several RSD directions/degrees vary among years. Incubation 26–30 d and fledging 28–32 d.
- **Limits/design use:** population statistics do not provide an individual standing-height ratio. Keep adults near equal height, female subtly fuller; do not apply a universal female-larger template. High confidence.
- **Links:** `research/species/86-otus-elegans-v4.md`; v4 concept/review; `production/runs/20261106T053000+0800-cycle-3.md`.

### ELE-AKATANI-MATSUO-TAKAGI-2011-JRR
- **Title/authors/year:** “Breeding Ecology and Habitat Use of the Daito Scops Owl (*Otus elegans interpositus*) on an Oceanic Island” — Akatani, Matsuo & Takagi, 2011, *Journal of Raptor Research* 45(4):315–323.
- **URL/DOI/accessed:** https://bioone.org/journals/journal-of-raptor-research/volume-45/issue-4/JRR-10-82.1/Breeding-Ecology-and-Habitat-Use-of-the-Daito-Scops-Owl/10.3356/JRR-10-82.1.pdf ; https://doi.org/10.3356/JRR-10-82.1 ; full PDF read 2026-11-06.
- **Facts/pages:** pp.315–316: 30 nests in introduced *Casuarina* cavities and two vertical broken fan-palm hollows; pp.316–319: shelterbelt/cane-field habitat and cavity measurements.
- **Limits/design use:** primary nest/environment evidence, not juvenile feather geometry or exact age. Supports a broad *Casuarina* cavity lip and Minami-daito mosaic. High confidence.

### ELE-BIRD-RESEARCH-NEWS-2011-P1-2
- **Title/institution/year:** “Ryukyu Scops Owl *Otus elegans*,” Bird Research News 8(7), 2011.
- **URL/pages/accessed:** https://www.bird-research.jp/1/seitai/8_7elegans.pdf ; complete printed pp.1–2 read 2026-11-06.
- **Facts:** p.1 gives overlapping adult wing ranges and modestly higher female mean mass for Minami-daito, same-coloured sexes, gray disc/rufous edge, yellow-brown iris, dark-gray bill, white scapular spots and bare gray-white toes; pp.1–2 summarize cavity/shelterbelt habitat.
- **Limits/design use:** institutional synthesis; primary studies control where available. Supports identity and conservative adult scale, not juvenile day-specific geometry. Moderate-high confidence.

### ELE-SAWADA-AUTHOR-GROWTH-2021
- **Title/author/year:** Akira Sawada author summary for Sawada, Akatani & Takagi 2021, “Growth curves of Ryukyu Scops Owl nestlings…,” *Ardea* 109.
- **URL/section/accessed:** https://sites.google.com/view/akirasawada/%E8%AB%96%E6%96%87%E7%B4%B9%E4%BB%8B/%E3%83%92%E3%83%8A%E3%81%AE%E6%88%90%E9%95%B7 ; sections “結果：リュウキュウコノハズクの成長” and growth figure read 2026-11-06.
- **Facts:** 99 chicks measured 2004–2008; at about 30 d/fledging, mass and tarsus were near adult while wing length remained in growth.
- **Limits/design use:** author summary rather than full article pages; no printed-page claim or new plumage geometry. Supports retaining near-adult body with visibly incomplete wing. Moderate confidence.

### ELE-ITIS-555377-2026
- **Institution/accessed:** ITIS TSN 555377, report read 2026-11-06.
- **URL/section:** https://itis.gov/servlet/SingleRpt/SingleRpt?print_version=PRT&search_topic=TSN&search_value=555377&source=to_print ; taxonomy/nomenclature lines 6–54.
- **Facts/use:** valid current *Otus elegans* (Cassin, 1852); synonym/original combination *Ephialtes elegans* Cassin, 1852; recognizes *O. e. interpositus*. Taxonomy only.

### Reuse, correction and unknowns
- Reused prior #86 v3 run/concept and repository-pinned Mikkola printed pp.160–161; uploaded original unavailable, so no fresh-read claim.
- New detailed sources5 / reused2 / corrected boundaries4. V4 preserves the age-unknown older juvenile but makes the research boundary explicit: body/tarsus may approach adult near fledging while wing length still grows; no exact-day plumage or flight claim. Adult scale uses trait-specific/time-variable RSD, not a universal female-larger rule. Exact individual age/sex, tract chronology, flight threshold, juvenile iris, standing-height ratio and fine toe geometry remain unknown; science false.



## 2026-11-06 — #87 *Bubo ascalaphus* v5 — adult identity, sex-size direction and c.40-day juvenile boundary

### ASC-ALSHEIKHLY-MIKKOLA-MOUSAVI-2020
- **Title/authors/year:** “Pharaoh Eagle-Owl *Bubo ascalaphus* (Savigny, 1809), the ‘shrouded in mystery’ owl of Iraq and Iran” — Al-Sheikhly, Mikkola & Mousavi, 2020, *Bulletin of the Iraq Natural History Museum* 16(2):219–230.
- **URL/DOI/accessed:** https://jnhm.uobaghdad.edu.iq/index.php/BINHM/article/download/473/317/1258 ; https://doi.org/10.26842/binhm.7.2020.16.2.0219 ; full PDF read 2026-11-06.
- **Pages/species/stages:** printed pp.219–221; adult *B. ascalaphus* field identity, adult wing series and desert habitat.
- **Facts:** pp.219–220 place the species in deserts, arid plains, wadis and rocky cliffs and distinguish it from sympatric *B. bubo*; p.221 reports short barred ear tufts, pale rufous-sandy plumage, orange-yellow eyes with a broad pale ring, dark-grey bill, prominent dark facial-disc line with white rim, abundant white throat, upper-breast-restricted streaking, clearer rufous barring below, and pale feathered tarsi/toes. Table 1 gives female wing 340–390 mm (mean 367, n=20) and male 325–368 mm (mean 346.5, n=20).
- **Limits/design use:** wing means establish female-larger direction at population level but not a rigid standing-height ratio. Show adults near equal visible height with female modestly fuller/longer-winged; do not exaggerate. High confidence for cited characters and sample.
- **Links:** `research/species/87-bubo-ascalaphus-v5.md`; v5 concept/review; `production/runs/20261106T061500+0800-cycle-4.md`.

### ASC-ALSHEIKHLY-2012-SANDGROUSE
- **Title/author/year:** “Breeding of Pharaoh Eagle Owl *Bubo ascalaphus* in Iraq” — Omar F. Al-Sheikhly, 2012, *Sandgrouse* 34:72–74.
- **URL/accessed:** https://www.osme.org/wp-content/uploads/2019/10/I_Al-Sheikhly_Sandgrouse34-1-compr.pdf ; full PDF read 2026-11-06.
- **Pages/species/stages:** printed pp.72–74; one approximately 40-day juvenile, adults, nest site, two later full-sized young.
- **Facts:** p.72 describes the c.40-day owl with creamy-sandy plumage, very pale newly developed flight feathers, developing breast markings, large yellow irides, pale legs and medium dark claws; its nest was a hole in a moderate-elevation cliff in an arid rocky wadi. Adults were pale rusty with barred lower breast/belly and a dark-lined disc around orange eyes. pp.73–74 separately document two full-sized young, so the stages must not be conflated.
- **Limits/design use:** the c.40-day age is an expert estimate; sex, exact feather count and independent-flight ability are not reported. Use a rock-supported older nestling with short pale developing folded wings, short tail and incomplete breast marks; no flight pose or mature remiges. High confidence for the photographed/described individual, moderate for exact age.

### ASC-ITIS-555406-2026
- **Institution/accessed:** Integrated Taxonomic Information System, TSN 555406, report read 2026-11-06.
- **URL/section:** https://itis.gov/servlet/SingleRpt/SingleRpt?print_version=PRT&search_topic=TSN&search_value=555406&source=to_print ; taxonomy/nomenclature and reference sections.
- **Facts/use:** valid current species *Bubo ascalaphus* Savigny, 1809; IOC 15.1 and HBW/BirdLife are among listed sources. Taxonomy only; the report lists no synonym. Historical *Bubo bubo ascalaphus* treatment is retained as a literature cross-reference from the 2020 paper’s lumping discussion, not as the current name.

### Reuse, correction and unknowns
- Reused repository-pinned `ASC-MIK2013-272` (printed pp.272–273) and v4 research/concept. The user-uploaded original was not mounted in this execution, so no fresh-read claim is made.
- New detailed sources3 / reused2 / corrected boundaries4. V5 applies measured female-larger wing direction conservatively, sharpens adult field characters and preserves the directly described c.40-day juvenile stage without importing the later full-sized-young plumage.
- Exact juvenile sex, exact-day feather chronology, flight threshold, numeric standing-height ratio, regional plumage variation and individual toe geometry remain unknown. Scientific verification remains false.


## 2026-11-06 — #89 *Bubo bengalensis* v5 — development, action boundary and old-name mapping

### BEN-RAMANUJAM-MURUGAVEL-2009-P519-524
- **Title / authors / year:** “Development of young Indian Eagle Owl *Bubo bengalensis*” — M.E. Ramanujam & T. Murugavel, 2009.
- **DOI / URL / type / pages / accessed:** https://doi.org/10.11609/JoTT.o1762.519-24 ; https://www.threatenedtaxa.org/index.php/JoTT/article/download/436/722/793 ; primary regional development study, *Journal of Threatened Taxa* 1(10):519–524, full PDF read 2026-11-06.
- **Scope/stage:** nine chicks around Puducherry, measured/photographed at 10–60 days; authors describe the study as preliminary.
- **Facts:** printed p.519: whitish natal down; prejuvenile moult around days 14–15; full juvenile plumage by day 35 and retained beyond day 60. Printed pp.519–520: after day 35 chicks could glide/flap away; day 60 treated as fully able to fly. Printed pp.519–520 and figs.1–3: wing/bill growth, early tarsal growth and a days 35–45 mass plateau are not synchronized. Sex differences were not tested (p.520).
- **Limits/conflict:** regional small sample; one measurement cannot establish exact age. “Could glide” is not universal individual sustained-flight proof.
- **Design use:** c.35-day full-juvenile-plumage bird on broad rock, closed short rounded wings and cautious grounded step; neither “flightless” nor fully flying.
- **Links:** `owl-pet/research/species/89-bubo-bengalensis-v5.md`; `owl-pet/design/concepts/89-bubo-bengalensis-v5.png`.

### BEN-PANDE-DAHANUKAR-2011-P1677-1685
- **Title / authors / year:** “Ecological effects on morphometric development of the Indian Eagle Owl *Bubo bengalensis*” — S. Pande & N. Dahanukar, 2011.
- **DOI / URL / type / pages / accessed:** https://doi.org/10.11609/JoTT.o2609.1677-85 ; https://threatenedtaxa.org/index.php/JoTT/article/view/676 ; primary morphometric study, *Journal of Threatened Taxa* 3(4):1677–1685, article abstract/metadata read 2026-11-06.
- **Scope/stage:** 16 Deccan Plateau individuals aged 1–58 days; wing, tarsus, claw, bill, tail and mass.
- **Facts:** traits show different growth patterns and are loosely coupled; comparison with southern India indicates geographic developmental plasticity.
- **Limits/design use:** no exact-day, flight or proportion inference from one visible trait; juvenile geometry remains stage-bounded and conservative.

### BEN-ITIS-555405-2026
- **Title / institution:** ITIS report TSN 555405 — Integrated Taxonomic Information System.
- **URL / accessed / type:** https://itis.gov/servlet/SingleRpt/SingleRpt?search_topic=TSN&search_value=555405 ; opened 2026-11-06; official taxonomy registry.
- **Facts:** current valid *Bubo bengalensis* (Franklin, 1831); synonyms include *Bubo bubo bengalensis* and original combination *Otus bengalensis*.
- **Limits/design use:** taxonomy mapping only; no morphology or development inference.

### Reused evidence and explicit unknowns
- Reused repository-pinned `BEN-MIKKOLA-2013-P274-275` (printed pp.274–275); the uploaded Heimo Mikkola file itself was not accessible in this run, so no fresh-upload reading is claimed.
- Adult sex cue remains near-equal standing height with at most subtle female fullness/longer folded wing-tail; no blanket female-larger rule.
- Unknown: juvenile sex, individual c.35-day flight competence, exact standing-height ratio, regional fine-plumage variation and precise outer-toe feather exposure.


## 2026-11-06 — #91 *Tyto javanica delicatula* v5 — c.40-day hollow-lookout stage and sex-cue conflict

### JAV-MCLAUGHLIN-DEBUS-2025-P116-123
- **Title / authors / year:** “Breeding biology and behaviour of the Eastern Barn Owl *Tyto javanica delicatula* in north-western Victoria” — John G. McLaughlin & S.J.S. Debus, 2025.
- **DOI / URL / type / pages / accessed:** https://doi.org/10.20938/afo42116124 ; https://afo.birdlife.org.au/afo/index.php/afo/article/download/2371/2402 ; primary field study, *Australian Field Ornithology* 42:116–124, publisher PDF read 2026-11-06.
- **Scope/stage:** 14 nests in semi-arid Victorian Mallee wheat-belt; 13/14 in tree hollows; estimated nestling period c.46–57 d.
- **Facts:** printed pp.117–118: recent fledglings retain mesoptile down and adult-like plumage; sex differences slight and unmarked birds were not confidently sexed. Printed pp.120–121: by c.40 d chicks ascend the hollow, look out, back down when fed and exercise wings; displaced chick climbed back using bill/claws/possibly wings. From c.46 d young may climb/jump/flap out and fly. Printed p.123 cautions against transferring other continental populations uncritically.
- **Limits/design use:** ages approximate because of asynchronous hatching and study fledging definition. Select supported c.40-day hollow-lookout/food-soliciting nestling with residual down, fresh folded wings and short tail; no flight claim.
- **Links:** `owl-pet/research/species/91-tyto-javanica-delicatula-v5.md`; planned v5.
